ParlaMint-DK_20210601130009	I would like to ask you to remain calm in the Chamber. We'll start with memoirs.
ParlaMint-DK_20210601130020	It is with sorrow that the Danish Parliament has received notification that former Prime Minister Poul Holmskov Schlüter has died on 27 May 2021. Our thoughts and compassion go to Poul Schlüter's family. With his passing we have lost a figure that made great impressions on Danish politics in the 20th century. He wrote himself into history as the prime minister who has held the post longest since World War II, and the only Conservative Prime Minister so far after the change of system in 1901. Poul Schlüter was born in Tønder in 1929 and became a lawyer in 1957. He was first elected to the Folketing in 1964 and was thus a member of the Parliament for 30 years until 1994. Poul Schlüter took over the post of Prime Minister on 10 September 1982 after Anker Jørgensen, and it became more than 10 years as Prime Minister. In 1993 he and the Conservative-led government resigned as a result of the Tamil report by Supreme Court Hornslet Mogens Hornslet. Poul Schlüter managed to lead four governments. The first consisted of the Conservative People's Party, the Left, the Centrum-Democrats and the Christian People's Party, the one we called the quadruple government. After the parliamentary elections in 1988, the Ministry of the Interior formed Poul Schlüter III with the Conservative People's Party, the Left and the Radical Left. And the last government under Poul Schlüter consisted of the Conservatives and the Left. Under the leadership of Poul Schlüter, the Conservative People's Party achieved unseen support. In the 1984 parliamentary elections, the party rose from 26 to 42 seats, and it was just before that Poul Schlüter was right in one of his very famous sayings, namely that ninety percent of the Danish population is conservative, but they just don't know it. The first part of Poul Schlüter's time as Prime Minister was characterised by a strict economic policy culminating in the potato diet in 1985. It and a number of other initiatives contributed to the equilibrium of Denmark's economy after the crisis of the 1970s, and there was a parliamentary majority behind economic policy. But in the field of security policy, the Conservative-led government often fell into a minority. Unlike the Danish parliamentary tradition, Poul Schlüter agreed to conduct a foreign policy that was not in line with the government's own line. This led to what we called footnote policy, in which the bourgeois government was forced to take a number of reservations in NATO. It was usually in the form of footnotes that explained Danish peculiarities. But there was a limit to his pragmatism. Hans own red line was crossed when a majority in the Danish Parliament instructed the government to ask American naval vessels if they carried nuclear weapons when they stayed on Danish territory. This would be a break with the previous understanding between the United States and NATO's alliance solidarity. Here Poul Schlüter set his foot down and printed elections in 1988 on a security policy issue, a choice which, moreover, led to his government being able to continue. For posterity Poul Schlüter stands as a handsome and talented tactician and politician. Hans maneuvering skills are legendary. As he himself said bumpy: Ideology is junk. But that is probably because he had experience from many years in Danish politics that ideological doctrines could hold negotiations and stand in the way of practical results. Those of us who have seen Poul Schlüter speaking in this Chamber remember him as a debater with wit, humor and charm. He was irrefutable optimist. This was also evident in his approach to politics. Characteristic for him, in his first New Year's speech as Prime Minister, he beat the positive strings. Suffocate the discouraged, the negative, the skeptical and the anxious. Let us now begin to expand and grow, let us now look at things more positively. I think that these words in concentrated form frame Poul Schlüter's silence. After the Folketing, Poul Schlüter continued five years in the European Parliament, where he sat from 1994 to 1999. I would like to ask the Danish Parliament to stand up and keep one minute's silence in honour of Poul Schlüter. Honor Poul Schlüter's memory! Thank you for that and we will continue with the planned agenda.

ParlaMint-DK_20210601131023	When I put forward this bill and also the next legislative proposal, L 188, which we are also considering today, on increased transparency in the Court of Justice, I expected, I might say, a rather more positive approach to the two proposals. After all, it is not every day that an impeachment is brought, nor is it every day that the accused wants to see more openness about a trial. Moreover, it is not every day that an accusation is made in public and in this context, with many prejudiced political opinions and accusations in relation to the accused, and that is my case. After the first and second readings, of course, I know that both this bill and the next one fall with something similar to a bang. Only the Danish People's Party and New Citizens vote in favour, and parties that normally talk about openness and insight into everything vote against. Honestly, hypocrisy can hardly end. The charge was raised in public and almost tempts you to say with pipes and drums, not least with direct television coverage and a lot of accusations. However, the defense must be conducted in small edited fragments soaked through by journalists before it comes out to the public. That is the consequence when the majority here in the Folketing votes against this proposal and the Court of Justice is put down. I still do not understand why it is so important for so many parties in the Folketing to fight against increased openness and real public access in impeachment cases. What are the Danes not allowed to see? I can say quite clearly, and I am, after all, the accused in this case, that I have nothing to hide. I know what I have done; I know what I have said, and I can measure everything. Court cases are special. It is a political majority who decides whether to make an accusation. The case is about the office of a minister and half of the judges are politically appointed. I am not suggesting that you should look up the doors and open the cameras to any criminal proceedings in the Danish country, but an impeachment on a particular subject such as child bride, which has been the subject of countless hours of public debate and discussion, is therefore not a common criminal case. That is why the public on paper, as the rules are today, is not enough either. Today it takes a personal appearance if you want to attend a case in the Court of First Instance in your own eyes and then there has to be some room. This will mean public access for the privileged, not for the many. For the truck mechanic in Holstebro, this is just not a real opportunity, because it is not a real opportunity to take some thirty in the autumn and then show up at Christianshavn and hope that there is space. So it's easier if you're a lawyer from Frederiksberg. Frankly, I am a little surprised that so many parties, who otherwise claim to fight for more openness, are fighting so hard against the Danes being given the opportunity to see what is going on in the Court of Justice. And they do it, I think, with one more agonizing hump and argument than the other. Perhaps one does not trust enough one's own accusation; perhaps one fears what openness can lead to. It is certainly not the media that is standing in the way. Those who have bothered to spend time reading the media consultation answers will also know that there is very, very strong support from that edge. If nothing else, it can be said that when this bill falls with a bang in a moment, it is quite the end of some of you, my colleagues here in the Danish Parliament, to argue that the media are distorting reality and refer only half of what has been said and that you may think that you have been misinterpreted by journalists. For when the Folketing believes that in an impeachment case there is an impeachment charge! I trust the Danes. I believe that public opinion is better than relying on edited fragments of reality in a case like this. Fortunately, I am not only with that view, which is why I would very much like to thank both the Danish People's Party and the New Citizens' Party for their support. These are two parties that are not just talking about openness as an ideal for themselves. I would also like to thank the very large section of the press who hold to their ideals. I am thinking here, for example. on the Dagbladet Information and Anton Geist, Dagbladet Politiken, TV 2 and DR, just to mention some of those who have supported the proposal and who highlight the importance of maximum transparency in a case such as this one, which has such a fundamental impact on how ministers can perform their duties. I have every confidence that the judges of the Court of Justice can ensure a dignified and reassuring settlement of impeachment cases. This applies to me, but also, if there were to be other impeachment cases later. And frankly, I am a little concerned that so many parties in the Folketing do not believe in the Court of Justice, whose President is after all the President of the Supreme Court to be able to organise the matter properly if there is a camera in a corner. Transparency is not an expression of disrespect; on the contrary, it is a declaration of confidence.

ParlaMint-DK_20210601141128	Amendments have been tabled and I can see that Mrs Ellen Trane Nørby from the Left wishes to speak.
ParlaMint-DK_20210601141146	Thank you, Madam President. Yes, I do, because we on the Left have tabled a number of amendments which are basically about restricting the powers that this bill gives to the Minister. That is because the draft law basically does not limit what data the Minister can obtain. I think that when you look at what powers have been used for the past year, everyone should look with care at when you just give infinite powers that ministers can do anything in such a way without having to have it anchored in a majority of the Folketing or in any way ask legislators whether the data planned for is something that a majority supports. That is why we have tabled some amendments which are basically about narrowing the authority of the Minister so that it is in line with what is needed, namely to obtain data that are comparable across municipalities, so that as parents we saw as politicians, so that we as citizens can keep track of what the quality of our day institutions is. In fact, I saw at first reading that there were a great many parties that also indicated that there was no wish for there to be such an open barn for what data could be obtained and for all possible purposes. It is even apparent from the comments on the draft law that the Minister can obtain data for political surveys. So, excuse me, does it create better quality in today's institutions if it wants to make political surveys at any time? I do not think so, and it must therefore be said that, if we do not restrict, there is a risk that this will create bureaucracy, bureaucracy, bureaucracy and take time from our children instead of being a tool that can help to support good quality in the day institutions. I hope that this can still be done, despite the fact that the report does not indicate that among the red parties there is a desire that we ensure precisely what was intended to be the bill is precise and specific about the data we need in the field of children, which is about quality and which is about being able to keep up with. On the other hand, I am very pleased that the report states that both the Radical Left and Uffe Elbæk support our amendment, which states that it is not fair that only the municipalities that the government wants to compensate financially for the burdens that go with the bill, but that, of course, the institutions, kindergartens and sfos under the free schools should also be compensated. Of course, the private and self-serving day-care institutions must also be compensated for the additional administration resulting from the bill. So thank you to Uffe Elbæk and the Radical Left for having indicated in the report that they would support that amendment. ParlaMint-DK_20210601142442	After all, this is about the implementation of an EU directive, which I myself have helped to reach agreement on after four years of negotiations and work in the European Parliament. It happened on April 17, 2019. The bill here is very good, because basically both the directive and this bill were about to do away with what I have called the world's greatest art theft, namely that there are companies that earn a lot of money from passing on other people's products and paying nothing at all to the artists or to those who publish the publications. So that frame is fine. There's just some vagueness in the bill. During the very, very long debates in the European Parliament and with the Council and the Commission, we had a very intense public debate, in which there was a great deal of activity online and also in relation to us who were negotiating this directive. Personally, in a 24-hour period, I received 73,000 emails from people who were dissatisfied with this directive, because the claim was that it would destroy the Internet as we know it today. I disagree with that. However, we did a great deal to try to land a text and a spirit in the proposal, which should ensure that we do not get a whole lot of legal proceedings. That is why there are two articles in that directive, namely Article 15 and Article 17, which are absolutely central and which this draft law does not address in its entirety. We promised at the time during the negotiations that, if you go to Strøget and take up yourself, it would never be the case that the music that then comes out of a store that might play music should be taxed. I think most people will think that it is not sensible to say that music used passively and indirectly must be taxed in an agreement system. That's not fair. It must be the direct use which must be taxed. If we do not clarify this scope, as it says in an English word, this framework, we will see a host of legal proceedings in the coming years, and none of the players have an interest in that end. That is why it is my recommendation to ensure that this framework is as precise as possible, exactly as we discussed it, and as we promised when we reached the final agreement on this EU directive. The second is about something called framing. Now, in case I take a picture from up here by mr. Martin Lidegaard then got his attention and Mr. Martin Lidegaard is my primary object, so is it so that behind there is a very beautiful painting of Olaf Rude, and should the painting that is in the background also be taxed? The obvious answer should be no, but according to the law here, it is not very clear. And it will also open up a lot of trials, and it will limit a whole lot of publications from being able to take pictures. In any case, they must be in and manipulated afterwards if you are going to stand in front of a poster or in front of a work of art, because in the background there is a work of art which then has to be taxed. That and the fact that we can pass on the so-called hyperlinks with a framework as long as they are behind a payment wall without any charges being levied for it, we need to secure that, because that is what we promised the citizens when we negotiated this EU directive, and these are the ambiguities that the otherwise excellent bill by the Minister of Culture does not address. In order to avoid any ambiguity and to implement the law in the spirit in which it was drafted under the directive and to avoid a lot of legal proceedings in the future, I would urge the House to vote in favour of the amendments that I have tabled outside the report.

To earth in the Sound, and to build a city the size of Hillerød. It is an 80 billion kroner plan which would normally be environmental impact assessment in its entirety, so that the Copenhageners and all others interested in the largest construction project in Danish history can see what environmental consequences the overall project entails. But we are not getting an overall environmental impact assessment. For while the Minister for Housing talks about urban development and infrastructure and 50,000 homes, the Minister for Transport only talks about land disposal and coastal protection. For it is the Minister of Transport who must have adopted the Construction Act so that you can make the peninsula. And if you pretend that it is only land landfills and coastal protection that you want to do, you don't have to make an environmental impact assessment of the whole project. On the other hand, environmental impact assessments can be made of the project's individual parts, so that citizens only know the overall environmental impact of the project when most of the construction is built and there is no possibility of rewinding the project. That method calls Professor Peter Pagh of the University of Copenhagen slicing, also known as the salami method. He has said in an expert hearing here at Christiansborg that this is an approach that the European Union does not accept and that we are therefore at risk of having an EU case if we do not make an overall environmental impact assessment of the entire Lynetteholms project, or at least what is called a strategic environmental assessment. He is not the only one who believes that Denmark is in conflict with EU law. Professor Ellen Margrethe Basse from Aarhus University is sure that the environmental consequences for Natura 2000 sites of excavating Lynetteholm and building a peninsula will be so much in conflict with the EU Habitats Directive that she bet a good bottle of red wine that Denmark would also lose an EU legal case in that account. Another reason for waiting to adopt the construction law for Lynetteholm is that, according to the Danish Hydraulic Institute, the installation of Lynetteholm will reduce the flow of salt through the Øresund to the Baltic Sea, and therefore, in the opinion of the Alternative, a consultation should be held with the countries around the Baltic Sea. The Danish Hydraulic Institute says that salt flow is reduced by 1/4 per cent, but there are scientists, for example. PhD Morten Holtegaard, who says that these calculations are highly flawed. There remain those of us who do not know anything about oceanography, and we do not quite know what to think. ParlaMint-DK_20210601144916	Thank you. Lynetteholm is one of Denmark's largest construction projects. But it is not only us in the Enhedslisten and the Alternative who are concerned about the consequences of Lynetteholm. So are our Swedish neighbours. They are extremely critical of the consequences for the marine environment and the changed seawater influx into the Baltic Sea, and they have said in this regard that the environmental impact consultation process, according to the Espoo Convention, is neither complete nor complete, as they do not feel convinced that the model calculations presented on environmental assessments are correct. That concern is now also spreading, and although the Danish Government has not thought it was really relevant to ask them, both Finland and Poland have now also asked for further information on the environmental consequences of Lynetteholm, while Lithuania has even asked for a consultation process to actually start with reference to the Espoo Convention. At the same time, the HELCOM cooperation, which aims to create a healthy marine environment in the Baltic Sea, has just announced that at their next meeting, Thursday and Friday this week, that is, on the same day as the Construction Act is to be third-read here in the Danish Parliament, they must also deal with Lynetteholm and its consequences. I think this clearly shows that the government is trying to push the process through. In the view of Enhedslisten, this is neither an expression of a democratic process nor an environmentally sound process. Therefore, I would also like to call on the Folketing to step on the brakes and ensure that L 220 is not rushed through until we have clarified the environmental consequences in cooperation with our neighbouring countries.

ParlaMint-DK_20210601145857	Three technical reviews at the Ministry of Transport, four consultations on the bill, one general consultation, one expert hearing organized by the Transport Committee and 188 questions for the bill. I repeat: three technical reviews, four consultations, one expert hearing, 188 questions. The examination of this bill has so far given rise to this. One might then ask oneself why I choose to carry further words to the pile by taking the floor here for the second reading of the legislative proposal. I do so because I believe that there is an answer to the concerns that have been raised for the project, including the concerns that have been raised here at second reading. No one can reasonably say that the matter has yet to be clarified. The level of information is so high that nothing should now prevent us from taking a political stance on the matter. And it is, moreover, whether you want to vote for the installation of Lynetteholm or against the installation of Lynetteholm, because of course you are also welcome to come to the conclusion that you will not vote in favour. Let me begin with the claim that it would be contrary to Denmark's EIA obligations if the bill were adopted. In this respect, reference is made to the fact that this would be contrary to the so-called salami method. In this connection, I can refer to my reply to Question B of 21 May. The conclusions of the Attorney General are that it will not be contrary to our EU legal obligations if the bill is adopted. The same was also expressed for the technical review we had in the Ministry of Transport yesterday morning. I therefore believe that this issue has been sufficiently clarified, and the government is therefore also sure that this issue cannot prevent the vote in favour of the legislative proposal. Then there have been questions as to whether a so-called SME should not have been carried out what is called a strategic environmental assessment. Questions have been asked about this in the context of the examination of the draft law. You have been answered in answer to Question No 1, by Mr von der Vring, on behalf of the Group of the European People's Party (Christian-Democratic Group). 29, In addition, Professor Peter Pagh noted during the Committee's expert hearing that an SME should have been carried out. And let me refer in particular to the fact that in my answer to Question No. 29 wrote the following, and I quote: the Ministry of Transport made back in 2019 an assessment of whether there was an obligation to carry out a strategic environmental assessment (SMEs) of the principle agreement for Lynetteholm. It was, and continues to be, the Ministry's legal assessment that the agreement is not covered by the relevant rules. In addition, I can also refer to my answer to Question No. 159 where I stated the following, and I quote: "I can state that this project is based on a political agreement which, in accordance with customary practice, has formed the basis for a subsequent environmental assessment process (EIA) and later on the draft construction law which the Danish Parliament is now considering. The Ministry of Transport clearly believes that, in this context, there is no obligation to carry out strategic environmental assessments (SMEs). This is backed up by the Attorney General, cf. The Attorney General's note of 25 May 2021, which was sent to the Transport Committee in response to question No. 55 to the bill. ▪ It has therefore been the Ministry of Transport's assessment throughout the process that there is no legal obligation to carry out a strategic environmental assessment of the 2018 principle agreement, nor of the subsequent construction law. This assessment is also supported by the Legal Assessment of the Attorney General. Let me now turn to another point which also raises questions, among other things. Here in the Chamber today too, Sweden and Espoo. A number of questions have been asked about how we can adopt this construction law in a situation where the Espoo process is not yet over with the Swedes. There has also been a question as to why there has not been Espoo consultation with other countries. To take the last point first: in my answer to consultation A, I was given the opportunity to answer why the other countries around the Baltic Sea had not been consulted in relation to the treatment of the underlying environmental base for Lynetteholm. In this connection, I had obtained an opinion from the environmental authorities, the Danish Environmental Protection Agency and the Danish Transport Agency, which explained the progress in a comprehensive opinion. The opinion is given in my speech manuscript, which I sent to the Committee on my answer to Question No. 169. It can be read here that it was the environmental authorities' assessment that it seemed quite unlikely that there could be cross-border influences in Germany and Poland when establishing a peninsula in the harbour of Copenhagen. We have also been informed at the technical reviews of City & Port, and not least by their advisers, that there may be some impact on Sweden in connection with the folding of materials in Køge Bay, but that there will also be no effects. I therefore do not see how it would adversely affect other countries when they are further away from Sweden compared to Sweden. However, that does not change the fact that I am aware that other countries have approached after they have received a letter of concern from a party in this matter, as Mr. Torsten Gejl mentioned sending. However, as I also indicated in my answer to Question A, information sheets have been sent out to the other countries. In relation to the question of the Espoo consultation process with Sweden, it is the Danish environmental authorities' assessment that the impact on Swedish areas is limited. In order to answer the above-mentioned consultation question A, I had asked City & Port to explain the environmental impact on the Baltic Sea if the Construction Act were adopted. In this connection, my speech was about the water flow in Øresund, and it was about Lynetteholm's blocking effect and clapping in Køge Bay. City & Port estimates that Lynetteholm's impact on the flow of Øresund in the short term and in the long term will have no significant effect on the marine environment as it is within the general variation over the years. In relation to clapping, I indicated that environmental impact assessments show that Swedish Natura 2000 sites will only be affected to a negligible extent as a result of the slap. As a result of the Espoo process and the dialogue with the Swedish authorities, City & Port has proposed the establishment of a special forecasting and monitoring programme. The forecast and monitoring programme shall prevent flapping of materials when in rare cases the direction of flow can lead to the spread of sediment to Swedish waters. Similarly, when setting up measuring stations, the program shall monitor the impact. A commission is also set up to monitor the development of sediment distribution. The proposal for the establishment of a forecasting and monitoring programme, in which the Swedes are invited to participate, is now set out in the implementation statement. As stated earlier, a copy of my speech manuscript has been forwarded to the committee. In addition, I would like to point out that, at a technical review on 25 May this year and yesterday at a new technical review, By & Havns counsellor, DHI, explained why the impact on Lynetteholm is minimal in relation to water flow and the impact from the slap. Finally, I also think it is worth noting that, in the context of the technical review, the third technical review yesterday, it was pointed out that there is experience of clapping from precisely the two slaps, including the impact of the slap. This is because the two folding spaces have also been used to fold material in connection with the existing soil depot in Nordhavn. As I heard from previous experience, it only supports that the assessment of possible impact on Swedish Natura 2000 sites is true. We will continue the dialogue with the Swedes and answer all questions as long as the Swedes need it. However, it is the overall assessment by Denmark that the environmental impacts are minimal and that the construction law can be adopted on that basis. As mentioned earlier regarding Sweden's concern, Lynetteholm's influence on water flow and salt concentration has given rise to concerns as to whether it has been sufficiently elucidated. I should also like to refer to my answer to Question No 1, by Mr von der Vring, on behalf of the Group of the European People's Party (Christian-Democratic Group). 12. Here it appears, among other things, that DHI has stated that "I and I quote: For the transport of salt, the blockage is 0.21-0,23%, which is offset by a seawater increase of 2 cm. The estimates given are based solely on the current in Øresund. Taking as a basis the current estimate of sea mirror increases corrected for the effect of land raised, the effect on water flow will be offset after 10 years and salt transport after 13 years by a recital alone for Øresund. The climate-related sea mirror increases will also affect the current in Storebælt and Lillebælt. Storebælt is of greater importance for the exchange of water, salt and oxygen between Kattegat and the Baltic Sea than the Øresund. If you consider all three Danish belts under one (Lillebælt, Storebælt and Øresund), you will find that the blocking effect on the exchange with the Baltic Sea will actually be offset by a smaller sea mirror rise than the above mentioned. It is therefore expected that a reduction in the effect will be achieved more quickly and probably after only 5 to 6 years. The estimates provided help to clarify that the effect of Lynetteholm is small, compared to the climate-related effects that occur in the coming years. Depth of the sailing path south of the Middelgrunden, the navel, will also have a small compensatory effect on the reduced flow due to Lynetteholm. All in all, it is assessed that Lynetteholm's influence on water and salt flow in Øresund, does not impair the surface water areas Nordlige Øresund, Køge Bay and Baltic Sea 12 sm, as there is no significant impact on either the ecological or chemical state. Lynetteholm is a reception facility that makes use of the surplus land created in connection with various types of construction in Copenhagen and the surrounding area. Today the land is driven from construction sites to Nordhavn. In the future, the land will instead be transported to Lynetteholm. There will be no more or less land due to Lynetteholm. ParlaMint-DK_20210601154614	Thank you. The legislative proposal for which we are now dealing with amendments to this second reading is about putting an end to, or making, an increased effort to combat insecurity in Denmark. There are essentially two elements to which there are amendments here. One is that we have put in place a security-building residence ban, and the other is that those convicted of nightlife violence must be prohibited from entering the nightlife for a period of time. This is a trade, for example. I think most of us remember the case of Lauritz, who was brutally attacked down Gothersgade here in Copenhagen, trampled on, thrown out in front of a car, totally smashed. Of course, violent men who commit violence in the night, violent men who mutilate a young guy like Lauritz, should not just be allowed to return to the nightlife. That's why we're setting up a nightlife zone where people like those who smashed Lauritz can't come. And we are suggesting that if you violate that zone, you must of course be able to be severely punished. Now there is an amendment from the Radical Left. What we are proposing is that if you have been given such a ban on entering a nightlife zone or a place where strong spirits are served after 8 a.m. 24.00, yes, the police must of course be able to punish you if you violate that ban intentionally or grossly negligently. Now the Radical Left is tabling an amendment to remove the part that is about gross negligence, so that the police must be able to prove that the perpetrator who received his sentence is deliberately violating this ban. We are talking about bans, which will be the sign, which will be published and in which we must have a reasonable expectation that the person who has been sentenced to such a residence ban for the crime committed will not enter such a zone. I do not understand why the Radical Left wants to make it more difficult for the police to prove that they are entering such a zone. I don't understand why SF and Enhedslisten support it either. But when it can obtain a majority, it is because both the Left and the Conservatives have indicated that they will support the amendment tabled by the Radical Left. So now it is the policy of the Left and the Conservatives that it must be more difficult for the police to ensure that a type of violence that has destroyed a young guy like Lauritz and has been sentenced by a court to a ban on entering the nightlife for two years is punished for violating the ban. I am not quite sure whether or not we have had a very thorough treatment, so it is a little wrong to say that I am unsure whether the Left and Conservatives have fully understood the consequences of this, because I assume that they have. In fact, however, I find it deeply problematic that it can bring together a majority, and then the Left and the Conservatives must explain why it is considered reasonable. I do not understand it, and frankly I am a little sorry that we are now going to water down this proposal, because I think it is perfectly reasonable on behalf of all the victims who are being attacked in the nightlife, and I think it is rather a pity, not to say very regrettable, that all other young people who live in the nightlife may now be at risk of these types being there because it is more difficult for the police to prove that they have violated their residence bans. So I think that is really bitter, but there is time for the second reading to bring it back to committee and then you can re-evaluate your point of view. Another key amendment has been tabled here. Right now, it's a little quiet, but we're not gonna go over there where they are. And there are no other people where they are. On the contrary, it means there's a replacement in this residential area because those who are resourceful are moving, and others don't want to be there because it's insecure. This is just an example of why we want to create a security-building residence ban, which is on top of the tools the police currently have in the Order of Conduct, and which must be used if there is an area where ordinary people do not dare to move and if there are not already other tools in the box that can be used. Afterwards we put it forward and then it was praised by the then Vice-President of the Left and the current political rapporteur, and I quote: 'It is never bad when the government implements the policy of the opposition'. We had a first reading this spring, in which the Left praised the proposal with one appeal, that the sanction for violating this residence ban was too mild, and now it has happened that the Left has changed course and is opposed and can no longer support the proposal and has tabled an amendment calling for a model which is almost completely identical to what the police already can, but which is actually very sloppyly formulated, and it must be understood that there will be parallel legislation where legal certainty is uncertain as to how the authorities should use it. ParlaMint-DK_20210601170212	As it appeared, it is the Minister for Social Affairs and the Minister for the Elderly's turn. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20210601170223	Thank you, President. It's a good thing we all got on board before we start this important debate. So thank you for good meeting management for the President, and thank you for a good running speed at the caller of the question. First of all, I would like to thank the authors of the question for the opportunity to discuss this important agenda, because there is no doubt that the transition to adulthood is one of life's most important transitions; a transition which for all young people involves major upheavals and new challenges, but also new opportunities. This also applies, of course, to young people with disabilities who need extensive help and support in everyday life. For these young people, it is often extremely practical for everyday life to turn 18 years of age and thus formally become of age. In addition to the upheavals which all young people encounter when they are 18, the transition for these young people also means an important shift in the framework of the aid and support they need. This is particularly true in relation to the support which can be provided under the Services Act, because while support for children and young people under the age of 18 is provided under child regulations and, in practice, to a large extent to parental authority holders, after the age of 18, aid must be granted under the adult regulations of the law to the young person who is now legally considered to be an adult. Especially for young people with extensive support needs, it can present some very special challenges when the framework for the help they need in their everyday life shifts from one day to the next. As I understand the question, it is precisely these young people and their challenges and the framework for the important shift that we are discussing today. In the question I am asked about the government's plans to prevent the transition to adult regulations from ending up with cuts in aid for young people who have approximately the same need for personal help, support, etc., before and after the age of 18. After all, the issue is part of an agenda which is also important to the government, for better coherence in aid, among other things. At the time of the transition to adulthood, as you know, one of the objectives of the Social Democracy's disability policy initiative of May 2019. That is why I am also looking forward to the debate we are having today. I should like to begin by making a few general comments on the conditions for the issue. Firstly, I would like to make it clear that the Service Act obliges municipalities to ensure that children, young people and adults with disabilities receive the assistance and support they need. We also have an appeal system to ensure that the municipalities comply with that obligation and correct any wrong decisions. These are, of course, well-known points, but they are nevertheless important in this context too. For, of course, the transition to adulthood must not mean that a young person with disabilities will not receive the help and support that he or she is entitled to or needs. Secondly, I think it is important to bear in mind that the range of interventions under child and adult regulations in the Services Act is different. Therefore, it is not always easy to compare the efforts made after two different sections of the law. The differences reflect, among other things: the fact that parents typically have a significant role to play in the efforts made in childhood, while the role of parents for the vast majority of young people is smaller after the young person has become of age. For example, for some children and young people with extensive help needs, part of the support will be provided in the form of help to cover lost earnings. As you know, it is a compensation allowance that parents can receive if they give up their work in whole or in part to care for a child under 18 years of age at home. In those families where parents receive help to cover full-time lost earnings, this will typically mean that parents account for a very large part of the help and support needed in everyday life, combined with possible support in day care or school. When the young person reaches the age of 18, the parent's role as a parent ceases, and the young person becomes of age as a starting point. Although parents will of course still play a role in the lives of most young people regardless of disability, this means, among other things, that parents no longer automatically have the role of primary caregivers for the young person. Therefore, the possibility of helping to cover lost earnings also ceases, and the municipality, together with the young person, will have to decide how to meet the young person's needs for help in the future. Here one option can be an aid scheme under Section 95 or 96 of the Service Act, where parents may continue to carry out a large part of the assistance for it. An aid scheme can be the right solution for young people who have a great need for personal and practical help to get through everyday activities. And the scheme according to §95 (1). 3 can e.g. be relevant to young people who remain very dependent on their parents, including young people, where parents have been designated as guardians of the young person due to extensive mental disability. However, the adult regulations also provide for a completely different solution. It can, for example, be done. be relevant to consider whether the young person should move in his own home with support and/or receive support under other legislation in relation to education or jobs. It may also be that the right solution is a combination of help in the home and an activity and association offer outside the home. The support possibilities are many and they can be combined in many ways. And precisely because support opportunities in important areas are different in the area of adulthood, it is not necessarily easy to compare aid before and after the age of 18. Having said that, it is of course still important to ensure that young people have a good transition to adulthood and continue to receive the necessary help and support, even after the age of 18. That is why I am also pleased that we have already taken several steps together to create a better and more secure framework for the important transition. In fact, as the question also shows, before Christmas we had a unanimous adoption of new legislation requiring municipalities to start preparing for the transition to adulthood at the age of 16. The new rules also require municipalities to ensure that the preparation is all around the young person and that the preparation takes place in dialogue with both the young person and the parents. It is my clear expectation that the thorough preparation and dialogue in the vast majority of cases will create a good common understanding of how support for the young person should be organised after he or she has reached the age of 18. We have also adopted in the previous parliamentary assembly an addition to Article 95, which ensures that young people who need it can be monitored at home at night for a number of years after they reach the age of 18. As you know, in response to the general challenges in the social field, we have also launched an ambitious evaluation of the specialized social area. Based on the evaluation, we will offer our solutions to lift the visitation and ensure that the right specialized offers are available. And, as you know, we have lifted the welfare economy significantly in the major economic agreements we have concluded since the change of government. Since the government came to power, we have therefore, with the support of others. the authors of this request submitted to the municipalities more than 3 billion. kr. The extra funds must ensure better welfare, including for children, young people and adults with disabilities. Finally, I would also like to mention that, with good inspiration from this Chamber, I have placed legal certainty and trust in the area of disability on the agenda, among other things. with the creation of a new partnership with the National Association of Municipalities, Danske Handicaporganisations and the Social Advisor Association. The partnership shall include, inter alia: draw up a common charter to address the issue of security and predictability in the help of citizens. It is my clear expectation that the members of the partnership can jointly support an increase in the quality of procedures and dialogue with citizens, which can also help to prevent young people with disabilities from becoming entangled in the transition to adulthood. I know that we have not turned all the stones or solved all the problems with the measures we have already undertaken. So, before I round off, I will come back to the issue which is at the root of today's debate, namely the question of what the government will do in the future to ensure that young people with substantial support needs are not affected by unreasonable cuts in aid and support when they are transferred to adult regulations. I am quite prepared for us to look at how we can further ensure the transition. However, it is my assessment that we need more knowledge before the government and I can give a good idea of what further action is needed. Therefore, before the summer holidays, I would like to ask the Ankestyrelsen to launch a study to give us a better insight into what is actually happening in the transition to adulthood for young people with extensive support needs. The study by the Ankestyrelsen will take place in two stages: first, they will look at practice in ten selected municipalities. Here they will both review a number of cases and conduct interviews with case managers, with young people and with parents in order to identify how the process of transition takes place. And secondly, they will interview selected experts from the Danish Social Protection Agency, including VISO, and the Ankestyrelsen to uncover their knowledge of the municipalities' work in this area. The experts will include: could contribute insight into what they see across municipalities of strengths and challenges in managing the transition. As I mentioned at the outset, I am pleased that today we are debating the framework for the important transition to adulthood for young people with disabilities. And, like the authors of the question, I am concerned about finding good solutions, also in order to ensure that young people with a high level of help are not caught up in the transition. And by that I would like to thank you for the floor for now.

Therefore, on behalf of the SF, the Social Democratic Party, the Radical Left, the Enhedslisten, the Alternative, the Left, the Conservative People's Party, the Danish People's Party and the Christian Democrats, I would like to make the following points: Proposals for adoption The Danish Parliament agrees that with the adoption of L 17 a sensible step was taken towards ensuring a safe transition to adult life for young people with disabilities after the age of 16. For some young people with disabilities and their families, the transition to adulthood has created great challenges. They have experienced a marked cut in the aid and support they have been granted under the Child Clauses of the Service Act. The Danish Parliament therefore urges the government to examine as soon as possible various concrete models for further ensuring that young people will not be left without the right help in the transition to adult life, including a model in which the young person can keep the help after the children's clauses until the young person has been granted after the adult clauses. The economic consequences of these models must be examined. The government will subsequently discuss these models with the Danish Parliament's parties. • V 139).

ParlaMint-DK_20210601175644	Thank you for the floor. And thank you to the SF for raising this question debate on the transition to adult life for young people with a physical or mental disability, because it is a very important debate that we are going to have here today. In fact, over the past few years we have been able to read countless heartbreaking stories about young people and their families, who have felt on their own body how the transition to adult regulations in the Service Act has torn their family life apart, and some young people with disabilities have been forced into a 24-hour institution because the aid and support granted under the Services Act has been drastically reduced. This applies, for example, to the 18-year-old and multi-disabled Simone from Sheepvejle, who from one day to the next experienced that almost all help was cut from the age of 18 and only because she was 18 years old. Because it wasn't because she had become more self-propelled. As the multidisabled, she still needs help with everything. In the past, Simone was able to get help every day of the week at any time of the day, but at the transition to adulthood, new paragraphs came into force, and this meant that her municipal aid hours were reduced from 67 hours to 22 hours a week. The only thing that had changed for Simone was that she had reached the age of 18; her disability had neither increased nor diminished. Unfortunately, it is not only Simone who has experienced losing vital help at the age of 18. And almost half of all cases of adult disability that land on the table at the Ankestyrelsen are flawed and end up having to be resolved anew. This shows that there are severe legal breaks in the area of disability and that there is a need to find solutions so that people with serious physical and cognitive disabilities are not left without the right help just because they grow up. As a young person with a disability, you should be able to rely on the right help and support from the municipality even when you are 18 years old and move to adult regulations. In the transition from young people to adults, there are many things that change in legislation, and therefore many concerns that arise from young people and the family • concerns about what the future brings and whether it is safe and also concerns about their security that help and support accompany. When it happens today that young people with disabilities and their families are deprived of support and help in the transition to adulthood, it is about help moving from development to some kind of maintenance. For example, it is natural for a child to receive educational efforts and help and support in developing skills to learn to eat or put on clothes, for example. This helps to ensure that the young person gets well into adult life, and the same help does not make sense in adult life, where skills should have been developed as much as possible. Therefore, there is a difference between the aid and the distinction between the child paragraphs and the adult paragraphs we understand. Nevertheless, it is incomprehensible that in some cases, for example, the difference between the children's regulations and the adult regulations is incomprehensible. Simone's case, to which I referred first, must be so large that it causes such a sharp drop in aid hours. That is why we need to take a comprehensive look at how we can ensure more security in the transition to adult life for young people with disabilities, so that aid is not drastically reduced overnight without changes in the life situation of young people. We need to discuss concrete models and, with the proposal for adoption we have here, we would like the Minister to examine some concrete models that we can discuss. For example, Danske Handicap Organisations and the Muscle Dwindling Foundation have proposed that young people should have the right to keep the help after the children's paragraphs until the young person has received a grant after the adult clauses. This may help to ensure a smoother and more appropriate transition. We believe that solutions of this kind need to be discussed and that the Minister of Social Affairs is making a thorough study of what can be done to ensure that young people with disabilities are not left without the right help, just because they are 18 years old. So, finally, I should like to welcome my good colleagues in the Conservative People's Party and say that they also support the proposal for adoption. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20210601182910	We are all aware of concrete examples that the municipalities seem to think that young people with disabilities are suddenly growing from needing their help. We've heard of Alexander who lost all help two days before his 18th birthday. He suffers from a muscle loss, and his condition, according to his mother, did not improve, rather, on the contrary. Yet Høje-Taastrup Municipality chose to terminate all Alexander's helpers just 2 days before he turned 18. We've heard of other cases in this area. All in all, it is incredibly frightening that you seem to have such a hard edge from the children's area to the adult area. So the examples are many when we talk about the lack of legal certainty in the area of disability, and when we talk about cash-thinking and counter-pavery in the municipalities. Something has to be done. The system needs to be broken down, and something new needs to be built up. That is why I and New Citizens want to give all people with disabilities a uniform treatment. For the conditions that apply to support and help, therefore, must be the same for the individual with disabilities, whether you live in Gedser or in Skagen. And if you move across a municipality border, you have to be able to take your help with you. You shouldn't start all over again looking for help in a new municipality. And the help you need to know for sure when your condition doesn't change. So it doesn't suddenly make you feel any better when you're 18; you don't stop having a disability, which, unfortunately, several municipalities seem to think. It is just another proof that the municipalities simply are not in a position to do the job. To compensate for the laxity and maladministration of the municipalities, we in the New Citizens' Party want to create a National Professional Council which will be responsible for providing assistance and services to people with disabilities. The Council must be set up in teams throughout the country so that there is never far to the nearest. And under this National Professional Council, we will hire teams of professionals to meet and assess the individual with disabilities as a whole person and not just a case in the system. It could be in relation to relatives, friends, housing situation, disability, job, education and all that in a person's life that is valuable and that is part of the overall picture. The same team must follow the individual all the way to the door, of course. It must provide the necessary assistance and follow up regularly to adapt aid to needs. It must be the end of a sea of municipal case officers where one hand does not know what the other does. And, of course, the individual teams must be self-organising and autonomous under the responsibility of the law and the coordination of the national council. Isolatedly, a new system based on a stronger professionalism in the procedure will become more expensive. There will be fewer changes in the Board of Appeal and the possibility for municipalities to speculate on giving people with disabilities less than they are entitled to be removed. Everything else equal will lead to more people getting more faster. But there's also another side of the coin. Simpler rules and more correct decisions will help the individual with disabilities to clarify more quickly and in many cases to a greater degree of self-reliance. And the family's financial loss in terms of lost earnings and lost income, when the municipality draws offers of help for long periods and the relatives for long periods must care for a family member with disabilities themselves, also gives a socio-economic loss and reduced income from taxes and charges. So overall, I am not actually convinced that it will be more expensive for society to create a better system to provide the right help for people with disabilities. It may actually end up with a win-win situation where both the individual with disabilities, the relatives and the rest of society get something really good out of it. So instead of thinking in boxes and instead of thinking at physical age, we need a system that looks at the individual human being and what the individual needs. It is a system where the individual is at the center instead of municipal coffers. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20210601185528	Thank you to Mrs Adsbøl for the reasons for the question. ParlaMint-DK_20210601185551	Thank you for that, President, and first of all a great thank you to the authors of the question, because the service law's helping arrangements are and remain important arrangements in the help we offer to people with disabilities in Denmark. Last week we also discussed the aid schemes in the group here at a conciliation meeting in the Committee on Social Affairs. Now we have the opportunity to do it again, this time in the Assembly. The aid schemes are for the citizens we have here in society who have a very extensive need for help. The purpose of the arrangements for cash grants for the recruitment of helpers and citizen-controlled personal assistance, i.e. BPA, according to Article 95 and Article 96 of the Services Act, better known as the aid schemes, is to provide an opportunity for holistic help for citizens with a comprehensive need for help. The arrangements are therefore aimed at citizens who are not themselves able to do many of the things that we other people take for granted every day. Imagine, for example. a normal everyday life where you have a social event in the afternoon, you look forward to it, for example. be a café ride with a friend or a friend. For a person who is in the target group for an aid scheme, such a café trip may prove to be an almost impossible task if you do not have a helper to assist in all the practicalities of the journey. How to pack the bag, get out of the door and be transported to the café if you have a large mobility disability and disability in the musculoskeletal system? In such a case, it is crucial that you have a helper who puts the needs of the citizen at the heart of it. The aid schemes are flexible schemes that can offer individually adapted assistance to the citizen so that the citizen can maintain or build an independent life. An aid scheme can meet personal needs and wishes of a practical and social nature which could not be offered by any employee. It is therefore essential that citizens have a permanent team of helpers who can decode the needs of the citizen in everyday life so that helpers know when to be on and how to withdraw from the background, for example. on a café tour. I think we can all agree that the aid schemes are of great importance to those citizens who have massive aid needs and who, on a case-by-case basis, need these schemes. The question here focuses first on the question of whether the personalities in the aid schemes have been narrowed. As I also mentioned at the conciliation last week, the government does not believe that it should be a struggle for people with disabilities to get help and no people should be left alone with unmet aid needs. If, on a specific and individual basis, a person meets the requirements of the legislation, then of course he must have the help that responds to the needs of the aid. A scheme under Section 95 of the Services Act on cash grants for the recruitment of helpers is and has always been aimed at citizens with significant and lasting reduced physical or mental capacity who need help with personal care and to solve practical home tasks for more than 20 hours a week. And an arrangement under Section 96 of the Services Act on BPA, as it has always been, is aimed at citizens with significant and lasting reduced physical or mental capacity who have a need that makes it necessary to provide this very special support, including the need for support for care, monitoring and accompanying. There have therefore been no changes in the rules which narrow the circle of persons, either for the relief schemes under § 95 or § 96. If you look at the approach to the aid schemes, understood as new beneficiaries in a given year, then the Ministry's figures, as I also mentioned in our consultation on the aid schemes last week, show that the approach to both schemes has decreased in 2018 and 2019 compared to 2017. Instead, if you look at the total number of recipients of the two aid schemes, you see a somewhat less dramatic picture. The total number of recipients has not fallen sharply. From 2015 to 2019 the number of recipients of § 95 has decreased from 530 to 490 persons, and the number of recipients of § 96 has decreased from 1,800 to 1,780 persons. Taking into account the cost of the two schemes, it is further apparent that the total cost of the two schemes has decreased neither in the years 2017 to 2019 nor over a longer period from 2009 to 2019. There are some variations between the years, but no tendency to steady decline. Overall, it can therefore be said that this is not a very significant reduction in the total number of beneficiaries of the schemes, or in the case of expenditure between 2015 and 2019, but that there are indications that the number of beneficiaries has decreased slightly in recent years, as well as signs of a decrease in the number of beneficiaries. Having said all that, it is also part of history that the figures for both the approach and the beneficiaries referred to are subject to a number of uncertainties, for example. they are based on data from only 45 municipalities and are listed from here to the national level. So we do not have the actual figures from the municipalities that can show us the full picture. In addition, the figures only tell us about the development of the number of beneficiaries, but do not say anything about the causes, i.e. what drives the development and decline in the recognition of new beneficiaries of the aid schemes. Is it the municipalities that have become more reluctant in the recognition of the schemes, as is also the underlying premise in the questions, or are there fewer citizens who comply with the requirements of the legislation to receive the schemes? We can't say that for sure. I have, of course, noted that there are organisations in this area which believe that this is the first, i.e. that it has become more difficult to obtain an aid scheme. I also note that, on the other hand, the municipalities tend to lean to the end, namely that in the past, the municipalities have been too generous in granting the schemes, a little harshly. The author of the question then asks about the use of available hours by the municipalities. I would like to make it clear that the legislation is very clear when the municipalities can measure these hours. For the municipalities, only in exceptional cases where the helper is present, but only exceptionally, can perform activities for the citizens measured in hours of availability. Even when it comes to the definition of hours of availability itself, the law makes it clear what is meant. Hours of attendance are hours where the helper is present, but only exceptionally provides assistance to the citizen. It can, for example, be done. be related to shared service or to a more dispersed need for assistance. It is therefore neither acceptable nor within the framework of the legislation, the hours of which are used to a greater extent than that. However, whether or not on-call hours are generally used to a greater extent than the legislation allows, we find it difficult to establish because of a lack of knowledge about the use of on-call hours. So we have no evidence to conclude that there are generally acute problems with the use of available hours. The third issue raised in the question is that helpers do not follow citizens in hospital. It is the case that the provisions of the Services Act are based on a fundamental principle of sectoral responsibility. The principle of sectoral responsibility is that the public authority providing a service is also responsible for ensuring that the same service is available to people with reduced functional capacity. In other words, it follows from the principle of sectoral responsibility that it is the responsibility of the health system that the citizen is offered the necessary and proper treatment and assistance during hospitalization. However, in all cases where a citizen with an aid scheme is admitted, a specific and individual assessment must be made of the question of the resignation of assistants. The municipalities must therefore assess whether it is appropriate to terminate helpers, taking into account that the citizen can maintain a stable help team during and after hospitalisation. But first and foremost, it will be the responsibility of the health system, and the fundamental principle of sectoral responsibility limits who should offer help when the citizen moves across the various sectors. Finally, I am asked about my view that everyday care and healthcare is being removed from the aid schemes. More specifically, the problem is that the time normally devoted to carrying out these tasks is no longer included in the measurement of the total number of hours in the aid scheme. Instead, the services in question are provided by home care or other health professionals in the municipality. This may help to make the aid scheme less flexible, because instead of being able to plan their own day, the citizen must wait for home care and, at the same time, it may be difficult to get the help guards in the scheme to hang together, as the measurement of time may become more fragmented. That is why it is already true today that, on the basis of a concrete and individual assessment, the needs of the citizen may well include assistance to ordinary everyday things which citizens without disabilities are normally expected to have done, such as: aid for health care. For the needs of citizens in the target group of BPA, according to section 96, both larger and smaller tasks spread around the clock. The assessment of the aid must be based on the purpose of the BPA and must therefore not be so narrow that the hours are only measured for help at specific times or for specific activities, so that for example. can not be measured helper hours for ordinary everyday things such as. health care. Because that would reduce flexibility and self-determination, which are, of course, key elements provided for in the system. As I began my speech with, the Services Act's aid schemes provide important help to people with extensive relief needs. The intention of the aid schemes is to provide a basis for flexible arrangements based on the needs of the citizen and self-determination, so that the citizen can maintain or build an independent life. However, when concerns are expressed by the stakeholders in the field about the aid schemes, there is a need to look at the issues, because getting the necessary help should not be a struggle. That's why I've started internal work in the Ministry looking at the aid schemes. The internal work will be prepared as soon as possible and will identify the major issues surrounding the aid schemes, including the issues of hours of availability, health care and the inclusion of helpers in hospitals. On the basis of the survey, the work will propose possible solutions to the major problems. In addition, we have also raised the municipalities' economy by more than 3 billion. kr. in the last two economic agreements, just as we have implemented a compensatory reform that ensures a greater redistribution from the well-off municipalities to the economically challenged municipalities, so there is better advice for welfare in all municipalities. We have therefore covered the demographics and more, so that the municipalities also have better space to give priority to helping people with disabilities, including that people who, according to a concrete and individual assessment, need a helper scheme can get one of these. In addition to the internal work initiated and the government's increase in the welfare economy of the municipalities, I have also already had a constructive dialogue with KL, Danske Handicaporganisationer and the Danish Social Advisor Association on precisely the problems with the aid schemes, when we had last met it was also the first meeting of the Partnership on trust and legal certainty in the field of disability. I hope and believe that it, and also future discussions, will help to focus on the purpose of the Services Act's aid schemes, so that all parties involved together can help to support that the arrangements are not undermined. For the purpose of the aid schemes is to provide a basis for flexible arrangements based on the needs of the citizen and self-determination, so that the citizen can maintain or build an independent life. I will continue to cherish that and thank you for the floor for now.

ParlaMint-DK_20210601194025	Thank you for the floor. First of all, I would like to say that I am pleased to be a co-author of this question, and then I would like to thank the other parties for helping us to focus today on how we can ensure that people with disabilities receive the help they are entitled to. The reason for today's query is the numerous examples of problems in the BPA area and how the BPA scheme is being eroded. These are stories about how people with disabilities have to fight an almost fierce fight to get support and help. In the Left, we believe that the BPA is one of the best welfare schemes. Today, there are more than 1,800 people on the BPA scheme, and with that scheme, 1,800 people will be able to participate actively in society and maintain social relations despite a very large handicap. With the BPA scheme, the individual is given the opportunity to hire helpers with disabilities, thus allowing the scheme to help determine how the aid is organised. It gives people with disabilities the opportunity to live a freer and more independent life; a life in which you do not have to live at the mercy of others and where you do not have the cashier's standards and one size fits all-solutions. In short, it is a model that gives power to citizens, which gives freedom to citizens, and we think that is actually a good Left policy. That is why we in the Left would like to support the BPA so that it actually works and that people who have the right to join the scheme will also have access to it without it being a long, tough and grueling struggle. Unfortunately, it just seems that the scheme is under great pressure. We can see that the municipalities have become more reluctant to give access to, among other things. The BPA arrangements. The number of BPA schemes allocated has fallen by almost 50%. over the last four years, although the number of people with disabilities is the same. If you step back and look at developments from a distance, it becomes clear that there are more problems at stake. One thing is that the approach to the BPA is decreasing; another and worse thing is that in many cases the BPA scheme is being watered down for the people on the scheme. For example, it has become clear that municipalities are increasingly giving so-called hours of availability to citizens instead of active hours of help. This means that people with disabilities have fewer hours where they can actually get help and several hours when they have to take care of themselves. The consequence is not only that the aid that comes with the BPA will be reduced; the consequence of the fact that there will be fewer hours is also that helpers with disabilities receive worse pay, which in turn means that it will be harder to recruit disabled helpers and harder for companies to be suppliers in this area. So we're well on our way into a vicious circle. A circle which begins with the deterioration of aid and ends with the complete disappearance of the basis of the system itself. In addition, the scheme is also associated with large-scale administration in the municipalities. We therefore need to have a look at the scheme, and that is why tonight's debate is also important. We live in a welfare society, and a welfare society is thus characterised by the fact that those who have difficulty are helped to cope with the challenges and to live a dignified life. I think we can all agree on that in here. But if we're going to end this trend towards people with disabilities getting harder and harder to get the help they're entitled to, then we're going to have to take the spoon in the other hand. Therefore, we also find it gratifying that the government has initiated an internal work and established a partnership with KL, Danish Handicap Organisations and Social Pedagogues to find solutions so that the BPA scheme is secured. However, I do not think I received a clear answer from the Minister as to whether the Minister will also include the parties of the Folketing in the discussions that are going to be. It actually sounded like it's gonna be a really long time before something happens, and that's too bad. There is a need for the Folketing too to be involved in those discussions so that we can find solutions together. That is why we in the Left, and indeed the whole bourgeois bloc, would like to call on the Minister to involve us in those discussions and to call for negotiations by the Minister. Frankly, I do not understand why the Social Democracy, and certainly not that the SF, the Radical Left and the Enhedslist will not be involved. However, I hope that the parties will reconsider that position so that we can get a majority in favour of some action actually taking place in this area, rather than just waiting and waiting. Finally, I would like to welcome my good colleagues in the Conservative People's Party and say that they also support the text of the adoption. Thank you for the floor.
ParlaMint-DK_20210601194535	Thank you to the Left rapporteur for the rapporteur's speech. There are no brief comments. And then we have to get the alcohol off before we welcome the SF rapporteur, and that is Mrs Charlotte Broman Mølbæk. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20210601194609	Thank you for that, President. The BPA scheme, the civilised personal assistance, also known as the disability helper scheme, is a very good scheme which allows citizens with severe disabilities to live an independent and active life because they can organise their own help and it will be on their own terms. Unfortunately, many citizens around the country experience that the scheme has been eroded and undermined or that the municipality will not grant it at all. And it has become much harder to get a BPA scheme. In the last four years, the number of new BPA awards has fallen worryingly. A BPA scheme is a cost-effective solution for the municipalities, and therefore several municipalities are likely to be more reluctant to grant it. Instead, home help is provided or citizens are offered a place in a settlement. But neither gives the citizen the freedom and independence that the BPA does. With home help, they are forced to live an institutionalised life in their own homes with fixed puttins and pee breaks, because their support needs are practically 24 hours a day. Many citizens also experience intimidating and cross-border controls in connection with the granting of BPA. Some citizens have experienced unnecessarily long and far-reaching observations in connection with the municipal assessment of their need for help. None of this is worthy of our welfare state. In addition to being reluctant to grant the BPA, the scheme itself has also been curtailed, curtailed and eroded for the citizens who have actually been granted it. This is among other things. the municipal subsidy for the administration of the scheme. In fact, the subsidy has been shaved so far down in several places that many of the proper management companies that help citizens manage their BPA scheme have to refuse to do the job. This goes beyond the quality of the assistance that the citizen can get in practice. In addition to being problematic for the citizen who has been awarded the BPA scheme, it has also created room for companies that abuse the scheme • not many, but we have seen some tired examples that you score the fund yourself, while both the citizen and the employees helpers pay the price in the form of poor help and working conditions. The municipalities have also eroded the scheme by generously granting the so-called hours of availability, where during these hours a helper is only available for small help assignments for the citizen and therefore receives less in pay for these hours of work. A higher number of hours of availability and fewer full hours of help limit the citizen and do not give the freedom that the BPA scheme is actually intended to do. In practice, many employees also find that the number of hours available does not match the actual need for the help that the citizen needs, and that the employee is helping, so that the work that is being done is not paid properly and it actually undermines the scheme. This limits qualified labour and, ultimately, also the quality of the support that the citizen can receive. The BPA scheme is a fantastic support option. Through the BPA scheme, citizens with disabilities and high support needs can be given flexible assistance. This gives them the opportunity to be more able to organise aid so that the generous support does not become a straitjacket, but instead ensures that the citizen can live an independent life on his own terms. Unfortunately, as I have said, we are seeing more and more examples of the reduction and reduction of the benefit so that the whole essence of the flexibility of the BPA disappears. And I hope that with this debate we will be able to focus on the issues of the developments that are currently taking place with the BPA system in the municipal visitation, and that the parties will indicate that the scheme must again give the freedom and autonomy to citizens with disabilities as it was intended. A rich welfare society like Denmark should be able to deliver on that.
