ParlaMint-DK_20210601200634	It has been said on the terms of the BPA disability aid scheme that there is state-authorized social dumping. The helpers are typically paid with a basic salary equal to the lowest in the municipal wage system; in return, the helpers do not receive all the pay supplements and other contractual benefits which apply to the professional group to which they are compared. It is obviously quite unreasonable to the helpers who carry out an important task in society, but it is at least as unfair to the citizens who are completely dependent on the help they receive from their helpers. There are municipalities which openly measure on-call hours in aid schemes, so that citizens are put in the quite unreasonable dilemma that they must either do without the help that is absolutely necessary for them to live a life on an equal footing with others, or ask their helpers to do the work underpaid. I believe that everyone can see that putting both the citizens and the helpers in is an absolutely unacceptable situation. We must put an end to this, which is why it is good that the Minister has made such a clear statement on the problem. It's just not quite done with it. There is a need to clarify very directly to the municipalities what it takes to comply with the measurement notice, as many municipalities do not seem to think that ministerial replies to the Folketing are something they need to correct. It takes even more than that. There is a need for an amendment to the Order which obliges the municipalities to grant the economy needed to finance that the helpers can be fully covered by terms which correspond to the terms of the same professional group to which they are compared. This is necessary if we are to stop state-approved social dumping and it is necessary if we are to bring the citizens out of the quite unreasonable dilemma I have described. ParlaMint-DK_20210601203925	Thank you to the President-in-Office for the floor. I would also like to thank the authors of the question debate for raising this important agenda. There is no doubt that legal certainty in the area of disability needs to be strengthened, because many people with disabilities experience that they have to fight a tough fight to get the right help. It shouldn't be like that. In a welfare society like ours, you have to be confident that the community grabs you when a physical or mental disability stands in the way of your being able to live an active and independent life. Trust suffers when people with disabilities experience that the system is not the good teammate who raises your hand and helps you up when you fall. Having said that, the municipalities have the possibility, within the framework of the legislation, of determining their own level of service. If, as a citizen with disabilities, you do not think that you will get the help you need and have the right under the law, then you have the opportunity to appeal to the municipality's decision to the Ankestyrelsen. We must not forget that there is also important legal certainty in this. Here there may be a tendency, including from us here from Christiansborg, to stare blindly at turnover rates in the complaints to the Ankestyrelsen. In fact, however, it is also legal certainty that you can assess your refusal of aid and have the decision reversed if it is contrary to the right of legal assistance. That does not mean that we are in line with legal certainty. I do not claim that. We can and must do better when it comes to legal certainty for people with disabilities, because getting the right help should not be a struggle. We have important work ahead of us to restore confidence in the area of disability, and that applies, as I see it, on both sides of the table. In this context, I would like to thank the many stakeholders, user organisations and political colleagues in the Danish Parliament... most recently Enhedslisten... who contribute ideas to concrete solutions to the challenges. This is an important input into the discussion on how we can together strengthen legal certainty in the field of disability. I understand that everyone is concerned about getting concrete solutions on the table too. That is why I am pleased that the government, in cooperation with the support parties, has already taken several steps to help strengthen confidence and legal certainty in the area of disability. People with disabilities must experience that the system exists for them and not the other way around. Therefore, the government, together with the Finance Law Parties, placed the Finance Act of 2021 on 30 million. kr. and 35 million. kr. annually up to 2024 which the municipalities can use for the recruitment of independent citizens' advisers. With a citizen counselor at his side, people with disabilities can get independent guidance and support to find the right help. The dialogue with the municipality must be a cooperation. Citizens' advisers are an important step in the direction of ensuring that citizens are involved and consulted. It is precisely cooperation that is essential to overcome the crisis of confidence in the area of disability. It's no use standing in each corner of our circle and pushing the blame for the challenges between us... we're gonna have to do the job together. Therefore, this spring I have taken the initiative for a partnership on trust and legal certainty in the field of disability, together with the National Association of Municipalities, Danske Handicaporganisations and the Danish Social Advisor Association. The partnership must be a forum for joint dialogue on issues in this area, where we commit each other to help restore confidence between citizens and the system. At the same time, we must remember that an economic crisis and a previous civil government that did not allow welfare crowns to keep up with the increasing number of children and the elderly have also put pressure on the municipal economy. I also recognise that this government has the ambition to rectify that. That is why we have already raised the economy of the municipalities by more than 3 billion. kr. in the last two economic agreements with the municipalities, just as we have implemented a compensatory reform that ensures a greater redistribution from the well-off municipalities to the economically challenged municipalities, so that there is affording proper welfare in all municipalities. We have also got rid of savings consultants in the municipalities, so that citizens are not at risk of reducing their help because a consultant who earns money from finding savings for the municipality has spoken out. In addition, nearly 100 million have been allocated over the last three years. kr. to reduce processing times and piles of cases on the Ankestyrelsen's desks. Having said all that, I agree with the premise of the question debate today. As I have already said, I believe that we can and must do more. The key to reducing the far too high conversion rates in the Danish Agency is that we strengthen the municipal case management. The Government has therefore put legal certainty in the disability field on the agenda in negotiations with KL on the 2022 Municipality Economic Agreement, which is currently under way. Here, too, it is crucial that we base cooperation on dialogue and the willingness to engage constructively in the work so that we can resolve the problems together. However, I would also like to give priority to legal certainty in autumn negotiations on the SSA reserve for 2022. Here I would like to help look at the possibilities of raising the quality of the case, supporting trust and cooperation and skills development. I look forward to discussing this further with Parliament's parties. In order to ensure better help not only for people with disabilities, but for all children, young people and adults who need specialized social interventions, solutions are needed that are thoroughly thought-out and professionally based. We owe this to the children, young people and adults who receive interventions in the specialized social field. That is why the government, in cooperation with Enhedslisten, SF and the Radical Left, has launched a comprehensive evaluation of the specialized social area, which must first and foremost provide us with better knowledge of target groups and offers. We will also analyse the basic structures in this area, including the distribution of tasks, municipal visitation, quality, specialisation mechanisms and financial incentives, all to ensure a thorough and solid knowledge base when identifying concrete solutions. In this context, let me stress that strengthening the specialized social area is a long, tough move. The evaluation will not solve all problems with a snap. It is also not measures that can be felt overnight, but on the other hand we are laying the foundations for a better organisation of the area, so that people with disabilities get the best possible help and support to live a good life, and I believe that we will succeed with it, even if it is ambitious. I would like to conclude by thanking the authors of today's question again. It is the government's clear ambition that we must act on the challenges and problems in the field of disability. Like the authors of the question, I am concerned about finding good and well thought-out solutions, and the government is already well underway, also with the Finance Law Parties, both with concrete and targeted measures to strengthen confidence and legal certainty, and with a comprehensive evaluation of the specialized social area. With that I would like to thank you for the floor for this round.

ParlaMint-DK_20210601205557	I think it is important that we do not make legislation on the basis of individual cases. It's a bad thing that rarely comes out of anything good. On the other hand, specific cases can help to clarify serious problems and shortcomings in our legislation. The so-called swim girl case is exactly such a case. The case is dealt with as it is probably all in this Chamber and also many outside this Chamber are familiar with a young woman with autism and visual disability who was refused escorts by her municipality at the transition to adulthood back in autumn 2014. After three years she finally succeeded in getting the municipality to comply with the law, but when the citizen then filed a claim for compensation for not having received the assistance she was entitled to under the law, she was unable to comply or, rather, said: she was entitled to the fact that the case had not been dealt with properly, but on the other hand all the courts, most recently the Supreme Court three weeks ago, concluded that she was not entitled to any compensation. It can be said that the only economic consequence for the municipality of the long-term case has been that the municipality saved money during the long period when, contrary to the law, it failed to provide the assistance it was obliged to provide. The lack of legal certainty experienced by citizens with disabilities over and over again is not worthy of a legal society. It is so unfair and unfair that citizens who are totally dependent on help in order to live a life like others can be deprived of help without having consequences for the municipalities that break the law. In the debate on legal certainty, it is often said that the most important thing is to get the right help for the first time. It is a view that I find it very difficult to disagree with. It is obvious to everyone if we start from the case I started with that it would have been better for the young woman to get the necessary escort for the first time when she applied for it back 7 years ago than to be awarded a compensation in the Supreme Court. It's self-evident. But that does not change the fact that it is extremely challenging for the general sense of justice that the municipalities can save millions on border-seeking, inadequate and erroneous procedures. It is high time we strengthened legal certainty, including for the many citizens who cannot comprehend to complain. There must be resources for a thorough examination; there must be independent citizens' advisers who can guide and advise citizens; there must be better alerting rules when a municipality wants to change aid already granted; there must be better supervision; there must be a legal certainty fund that can remove the financial incentive that is actually the reason why the municipalities' border-seeking procedures are carried out. We need to correct legal certainty for citizens with disabilities, and that is not least, given that in the autumn we are going to start negotiations on the reform of the disability area once we have completed the entire evaluation of the specialized social area. The many shortcomings and shortcomings in decisions in disability cases are a major reason why there is a serious gap of confidence which has developed between many citizens with disabilities and their relatives and the public authorities. Strengthening legal certainty is therefore essential if citizens and their relatives are to be confident that the reform that we hope will soon take place not only alters the formal ability of citizens to get the right help, but that they can actually count on the help they are entitled to under the law. That is why I am also pleased that the Minister for Social Affairs has promised to invite discussions to improve legal certainty after the summer break. I look forward to having a very good discussion, on which the Enhedslisten will be very insistent not to remain in the debate, but also to finding solutions with very concrete changes in the area of legal certainty. Finally, I shall read out a proposal for adoption on behalf of the Social Democratic Party, the SF, the Radical Left, the Enhedslisten and the Alternative. It sounds like this: Suggested adoption The Danish Parliament notes that both the many changes in municipal decisions made by the Ankestyrelsen and the Danish Agency's practice studies in the field of disability demonstrate that there is a great need to strengthen legal certainty for citizens with disabilities. The Danish Parliament notes that the Minister for Social Affairs and the Elderly has undertaken to convene discussions on how to improve legal certainty. The discussions will include: build on the partnership between the government, KL, Danske Handicaporganisationer and the Danish Social Advisor Association. The starting point for the discussions will be that changes to strengthen legal certainty will be implemented as soon as possible. Strengthening legal certainty must be seen in the context of discussions on the future organisation of the disability area under the auspices of the evaluation of the specialized social area. • V 142).

ParlaMint-DK_20210601220718	Thank you very much. By the end of 2020, 408 Danes were waiting for a new body, and 18 died last year, waiting in vain for a new body. Of the 408 Danes on the waiting list, 368 are waiting for a kidney. In all the years when statistics have been made, kidney disease is the vast majority of chronically ill Danes waiting for a new body to get their lives back. Therefore, if you could transplant more kidney disease with a new organ, you could significantly reduce the waiting list. It is not possible to meet the need for kidney transplantation with organs from the deceased. A kidney transplant is the best and cheapest treatment for patients with terminal renal failure, and since it is generally born with two kidneys but can live with a single one, it is possible to use live donors for kidney donations. More kidney donations from living donors will increase the number of annual kidney transplants, and the transplantation of a kidney from a living donor at the same time provides better health and therapeutic results for the recipient than a kidney from a deceased. 3 of the 23 initiatives in the 2014 Action Plan are therefore also targeted live kidney donation, but although they are assessed as being fulfilled in the evaluation of the 2017 Action Plan, it has not had the desired effect on the donor rate for live kidney donors. That is why we need a special effort and that is why we have addressed this question to the Minister. I look forward to hearing the Minister's reply. Thank you.
ParlaMint-DK_20210601220944	Thank you to Mrs Liselott Blixt and we will have the answer in a moment. The Minister of Health.
ParlaMint-DK_20210601221002	Thank you for that, President, and thank you to the Danish People's Party for highlighting with this question how we can strengthen the possibility of transplants of organs and stem cells. There is still a need to focus on how we ensure sufficient donors to enable vital transplants, and when we talk about organ donations, I will focus on the kidneys. It is the kidneys that we have the greatest need to secure organ donors for. The question here is, of course, about living donors, and this means, among other things, that a donation may involve a risk to the donor and there may be both physical and psychological health implications. In a Danish context we have not detected deaths in connection with the donation of a kidney, but if you look at the international figures, the statistics show that about three deaths occur at 10,000 donations. Therefore, it makes good sense that the majority of donations come from deceased people. By 2020 there have been 124 kidney donations from deceased donors, and that is more than ever before. The donor rate, that is, the number of donors in relation to the population, had actually also increased by 2020, and in fact it was in 2020 in Denmark higher than what they have in Sweden and Norway. But at the same time, unfortunately, there have also been fewer donations from living donors. The figures show that overall there were 278 kidney donations in 2020 compared to 249 in 2014, when the national action plan was drawn up, but there are thus annual fluctuations, and the waiting lists for a new one are still far too long. As things stand today, it is not possible to meet the need for kidney transplants with organs from deceased donors. Donation from living donors is therefore an important focus area, and efforts to increase the number of living donors have also been strengthened in recent years, and in 2019, for example, it was. possible to donate anonymously. Unfortunately, this has not yet led to donations, but there are three people currently in a study process on this. In 2019 a special Scandinavian kidney exchange programme was also introduced using so-called exchange chains, which means that kidney exchange from living donors can take place between two or more donor and recipient pairs, and the programme has contributed eight donations to Danish patients in 2019 and 2020. At the same time, there are a number of efforts to increase the potential for donations from the deceased. Currently, the Danish Health Authority and the professional environments, for example. by drawing up a report with professional guidelines after circular death, what is also called cardiac death, and the Danish Health Service considers that if it is introduced it could have a major impact on the donor rate. Finally, I would also like to mention that targeted information efforts are being carried out on an ongoing basis. The question today is precisely about information and how we ensure that more citizens are informed about the possibility of being living donors, and whether there is a need for new initiatives and, if so, an action plan. When we look at organ donations, patients and relatives at all transplant centers are informed of the possibility of being transplanted with a kidney from a living donor, and patient associations also report the possibility of living kidney donation. But the question is, of course, whether there is sufficient information and whether there is a need for further action here, and we will therefore have a clearer answer to that when the Danish Health Authority has completed the cover they are now implementing with the transplant centres. The cover shall include: make us wiser on differences in waiting times, the background for differences and a possible need for new initiatives, and focus is on kidney donation, but the Danish Health Authority will further assess whether initiatives are also needed in relation to other elements. In conclusion, I would also like to put a few words on stem cells and bone marrow. Each year, more than 150 Danes are diagnosed with a disease requiring a stem cell transplant to survive. It can also be very difficult to find an appropriate stem cell donor here, as the donor's and the patient's tissue types must be completely identical, and therefore it is important that as many as possible sign up for the stem cell register and are ready to donate if a match appears. Today, the procurement of stem cells is usually a relatively small intervention compared with the previous one, because we have a technology that has been substantially changed and significantly improved. However, it is the Board of Patient Safety's immediate estimate that many do not know how relatively simple it is with the current technology to donate stem cells and that there may be potential to recruit significantly more donors. At the same time, the board informs us that a national web-based solution is currently being worked intensively on trying to reach a wider level in the recruitment of stem cell donors, as well as that the Danish stem cell registers, together with the blood donors in Denmark in the autumn of 2021, have planned a national promotional campaign to draw attention to the possibility of registering as a stem cell donor, and Health.dk will also advertise the registration as a stem cell donor on the website. I cannot say at this stage whether that is enough. I would also like to ask the board to look into whether there is a need to set up even more activities. Then, when I stand here and we are talking about organ donation, I would like to say more generally that today, this morning, we opened up the new digital health card as an app, and I can see that it is already number one on the top list of the App Store. So the Danes have taken it to heart, and when the Danes go in and log on to what I have done myself, a screen actually comes up, where I think the modern word is nudging • gets information about how to become an organ donor. The Folketing recognises that kidney disease constitutes the majority of Danes on the waiting list. Transplanting more kidney disease will help reduce the waiting list/increase survival and improve the quality of life for more patients. The Danish Parliament notes that the Danish Health Authority is uncovering the area of kidney transplantation, including assessing the need to strengthen efforts to ensure an increased number of living donors. On this basis, the Danish Parliament instructs the government to discuss before the end of 2021 the need to strengthen efforts for living kidney donation with the parties of the Danish Parliament, for example. through increased focus on anonymous kidney donation, more support for finding donor opportunities as well as more information on the possibility of becoming a living kidney donor. The Folketing urges the government to clarify at the same time whether an action plan is needed for living donors of liver and bone marrow/strom cells. • V 144).

There are, frankly, only pluses associated with the fact that more sick people are being offered the necessary organs. Obviously, the increase in the quality of life associated with, for a kidney patient, is not to have to live a life of dialysis at great personal costs for the individual and for his or her relatives. In addition, it is, of course, linked to a socio-economic gain in terms of less spending on health care, and then to a group of people who can live a more effective life. There were three initiatives targeted at the issue of living donors, and it must be noted at least that there has been a lack of development in the kidney sector in relation to living donations. ParlaMint-DK_20210601223551	Thank you for the floor, which I am speaking on behalf of our usual rapporteur, who has been given early notice. In 2024 we will be able to celebrate the 70th anniversary of the first kidney transplant in the world. It took place in Boston in 1954 between identical twins, and 10 years later the doctors at Aarhus Municipal Hospital could give Grethe Mogensen a new kidney • as the first Dane. At that time, 50 transplants had been made worldwide. Last year, 278 kidneys were transplanted in Denmark alone. And almost 3,700 Danes have had a new kidney since Grethe Mogensen in 1964; most from patients who have donated their kidney at their death, but about a third come from living donors, mainly near family. Why do I say that? Because medical progress has saved lives and gave thousands of Danes better lives, and because progress almost always means increased demand. That is why we can also celebrate this year the tenth anniversary of Parliament's first decision to remove the waiting list for kidney transplants, to take that as an example. The list has not become shorter, and today, as several have mentioned, there is still a waiting list of 368. That is why we must do everything we can to reduce both waiting times and waiting lists, which is why I am pleased that there is a broad text here which will enable us to examine in the autumn what the Danish Health Authority finds of opportunities and initiatives to promote that agenda.
